========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, um… I came in because I just can’t seem to be still. It’s like I got a motor running inside me all day, from when I’m up until I’m trying to sit down somewhere. If I’m at church, or sitting here at the clinic waiting, or even just at home, I start feeling trapped in my own body, like I gotta get up, move around, mess with something, do something.

It’s not just being bored, you know? It feels uncomfortable, like pressure building up if I have to stay sitting. I’ll tell myself, “Just sit there, just be still,” but then my legs want to move or I feel like I need to stand up, walk, go outside, anything. It’s been wearing me out, and I figured I better come talk to somebody because it’s all day, every day kind of feeling.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yeah, it is. I’m not working right now, but it still affects me bad with people. Like at church, and at the pantry, and with my own family, people used to ask me to help with rides, or getting somebody to an appointment, or picking up supplies, stuff like that. And I want to help, I really do, but I guess I’ve messed it up enough times that now folks don’t really trust me with it.

It’s like they’ll say, “No, we got somebody else,” or they won’t ask me anymore, and I can feel it. My family too — they’ll kind of go around me when something important needs doing. That hurts, because I don’t mean to let anybody down. I say yes because I want to be useful, but then somehow it turns into me being the one who didn’t come through, and everybody’s frustrated.

So yeah, it’s affecting relationships. I feel like I keep disappointing people even when my heart is in the right place.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s a lot of distress, honestly. Like… it ain’t just “I’m a little uncomfortable.” It feels like something is crawling under my skin, or like there’s a motor in me that won’t shut off. If I’m sitting at church, or waiting here at the clinic, or even just trying to sit at home, I start feeling trapped in my own body.

I can try to tell myself, “Stay still, don’t make a big deal out of it,” but the longer I have to sit there, the worse it gets. My legs start wanting to move, I start looking for a reason to stand up, or I’ll feel like I need to go outside or mess with something just to take the pressure off. It builds up until it’s all I can think about.

So yeah, it bothers me bad. It makes me feel tense and frustrated, like I’m fighting myself just to do something simple like sit still.

I’ll tell myself, “Just sit there, just be still,” but then my legs want to move or I feel like I need to stand up, walk, go outside, anything. I’m not working right now, but it still affects me bad with people. Like at church, and at the pantry, and with my own family, people used to ask me to help with rides, or getting somebody to an appointment, or picking up supplies, stuff like that. And I want to help, I really do, but I guess I’ve messed it up enough times that now folks don’t really trust me with it.\n\nIt’s like they’ll say, “No, we got somebody else,” or they won’t ask me anymore, and I can feel it. My family too — they’ll kind of go around me when something important needs doing. That hurts, because I don’t mean to let anybody down. I say yes because I want to be useful, but then somehow it turns into me being the one who didn’t come through, and everybody’s frustrated.\n\nSo yeah, it’s affecting relationships. I feel like I keep disappointing people even when my heart is in the right place.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to move around or be still?\n\nPatient: It’s a lot of distress, honestly. Like… it ain’t just “I’m a little uncomfortable.” It feels like something is crawling under my skin, or like there’s a motor in me that won’t shut off. If I’m sitting at church, or waiting here at the clinic, or even just trying to sit at home, I start feeling trapped in my own body.\n\nI can try to tell myself, “Stay still, don’t make a big deal out of it,” but the longer I have to sit there, the worse it gets. My legs start wanting to move, I start looking for a reason to stand up, or I’ll feel like I need to go outside or mess with something just to take the pressure off. It builds up until it’s all I can think about.\n\nSo yeah, it bothers me bad. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. Not like some big ritual or anything, but I’m always doing little things over and over without even meaning to. Like even when I’m trying to sit there and be respectful — like in church, or sitting across from somebody talking — my leg will be bouncing the whole time. And I mean the whole time, until somebody looks at it or I notice the chair shaking.

Or my fingers will be tapping on the arm of the chair, or on my knee, or I’ll be picking at something, twisting something in my hands, messing with a cap or a piece of paper or whatever I can get hold of. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until someone says, “Can you stop that?” And then I’ll stop for a minute, but it’s like my body finds another way to move. If the leg stops, then the fingers start. If the fingers stop, I’m twisting my hands or rubbing them together.

It’s embarrassing because I really am trying not to bother nobody. I’m not doing it to be rude. It’s just like I can’t keep my body quiet.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… I don’t think so. I mean, not like that. It’s not like I’m thinking, “If I don’t tap my fingers, something bad’s gonna happen,” or I’m trying to fix something in my head.

It’s more like… my body just does it. Like pressure building up, and the movement lets a little of it out. I’m not trying to make anything right, really. I’m not counting or doing it a certain number of times or anything like that. I’ll just notice I’m bouncing my leg or rubbing my hands, and if I try to stop, then I get all tense and uncomfortable.

So no, I don’t think it’s about preventing something. It’s more just… I can’t seem to keep still.

It’s just like I can’t keep my body quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive movements because you're trying to make something right or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not like that. It’s not like I’m thinking, “If I don’t tap my fingers, something bad’s gonna happen,” or I’m trying to fix something in my head.\n\nIt’s more like… my body just does it. Like pressure building up, and the movement lets a little of it out. I’m not trying to make anything right, really. I’m not counting or doing it a certain number of times or anything like that. I’ll just notice I’m bouncing my leg or rubbing my hands, and if I try to stop, then I get all tense and uncomfortable.\n\nSo no, I don’t think it’s about preventing something. It’s more just… I can’t seem to keep still.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel anxious or worried when you're unable to move around or be still?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 36 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Feeling_Restless\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I feel like I got a motor running inside me all day, and if I have to sit still at church, at the clinic, or at home, I feel trapped and have to get up or do something.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do get anxious, but it’s tied to that stuck feeling, you know? Like if I’m not able to move around or get up, it starts feeling like I’m trapped. That’s really why I came in, because it feels like I got a motor running inside me all day, and it don’t shut off just because I’m supposed to be sitting still.

Like at church, if everybody’s sitting quiet and I know I’m supposed to stay put, I start feeling all tight inside. Same thing here at the clinic, waiting around, or even at home if I’m trying to just sit and relax. It’s not relaxing to me. The longer I have to be still, the more worked up I get, and then I start thinking, “I need to stand up, I need to walk, I need to do something.” If I can’t, then yeah, I get worried and tense, like I’m gonna crawl out of my own skin.

So it’s not always worry like I’m scared of something bad happening. It’s more like my body gets so restless that my mind starts getting anxious with it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F42.2, F44.9, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I just can’t seem to be still. It’s like I got a motor running inside me all day, from when I’m up until I’m trying to sit down somewhere. If I’m at church, or sitting here at the clinic waiting, or even just at home, I start feeling trapped in my own body, like I gotta get up, move around, mess with something, do something.\n\nIt’s not just being bored, you know? It feels uncomfortable, like pressure building up if I have to stay sitting. I’ll tell myself, “Just sit there, just be still,” but then my legs want to move or I feel like I need to stand up, walk, go outside, anything. It’s been wearing me out, and I figured I better come talk to somebody because it’s all day, every day kind of feeling.\n\nDoctor: Is this restlessness and need to move affecting your work or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it is. I’m not working right now, but it still affects me bad with people. Like at church, and at the pantry, and with my own family, people used to ask me to help with rides, or getting somebody to an appointment, or picking up supplies, stuff like that. And I want to help, I really do, but I guess I’ve messed it up enough times that now folks don’t really trust me with it.\n\nIt’s like they’ll say, “No, we got somebody else,” or they won’t ask me anymore, and I can feel it. My family too — they’ll kind of go around me when something important needs doing. That hurts, because I don’t mean to let anybody down. I say yes because I want to be useful, but then somehow it turns into me being the one who didn’t come through, and everybody’s frustrated.\n\nSo yeah, it’s affecting relationships. I feel like I keep disappointing people even when my heart is in the right place.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to move around or be still?\n\nPatient: It’s a lot of distress, honestly. Like… it ain’t just “I’m a little uncomfortable.” It feels like something is crawling under my skin, or like there’s a motor in me that won’t shut off. If I’m sitting at church, or waiting here at the clinic, or even just trying to sit at home, I start feeling trapped in my own body.\n\nI can try to tell myself, “Stay still, don’t make a big deal out of it,” but the longer I have to sit there, the worse it gets. My legs start wanting to move, I start looking for a reason to stand up, or I’ll feel like I need to go outside or mess with something just to take the pressure off. It builds up until it’s all I can think about.\n\nSo yeah, it bothers me bad. It makes me feel tense and frustrated, like I’m fighting myself just to do something simple like sit still.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not like some big ritual or anything, but I’m always doing little things over and over without even meaning to. Like even when I’m trying to sit there and be respectful — like in church, or sitting across from somebody talking — my leg will be bouncing the whole time. And I mean the whole time, until somebody looks at it or I notice the chair shaking.\n\nOr my fingers will be tapping on the arm of the chair, or on my knee, or I’ll be picking at something, twisting something in my hands, messing with a cap or a piece of paper or whatever I can get hold of. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until someone says, “Can you stop that?” And then I’ll stop for a minute, but it’s like my body finds another way to move. If the leg stops, then the fingers start. If the fingers stop, I’m twisting my hands or rubbing them together.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I really am trying not to bother nobody. I’m not doing it to be rude. It’s just like I can’t keep my body quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive movements because you're trying to make something right or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not like that. It’s not like I’m thinking, “If I don’t tap my fingers, something bad’s gonna happen,” or I’m trying to fix something in my head.\n\nIt’s more like… my body just does it. Like pressure building up, and the movement lets a little of it out. I’m not trying to make anything right, really. I’m not counting or doing it a certain number of times or anything like that. I’ll just notice I’m bouncing my leg or rubbing my hands, and if I try to stop, then I get all tense and uncomfortable.\n\nSo no, I don’t think it’s about preventing something. It’s more just… I can’t seem to keep still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried when you're unable to move around or be still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get anxious, but it’s tied to that stuck feeling, you know? Like if I’m not able to move around or get up, it starts feeling like I’m trapped. That’s really why I came in, because it feels like I got a motor running inside me all day, and it don’t shut off just because I’m supposed to be sitting still.\n\nLike at church, if everybody’s sitting quiet and I know I’m supposed to stay put, I start feeling all tight inside. Same thing here at the clinic, waiting around, or even at home if I’m trying to just sit and relax. It’s not relaxing to me. The longer I have to be still, the more worked up I get, and then I start thinking, “I need to stand up, I need to walk, I need to do something.” If I can’t, then yeah, I get worried and tense, like I’m gonna crawl out of my own skin.\n\nSo it’s not always worry like I’m scared of something bad happening. It’s more like my body gets so restless that my mind starts getting anxious with it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like if I’m not able to move around or get up, it starts feeling like I’m trapped. That’s really why I came in, because it feels like I got a motor running inside me all day, and it don’t shut off just because I’m supposed to be sitting still.\n\nLike at church, if everybody’s sitting quiet and I know I’m supposed to stay put, I start feeling all tight inside. Same thing here at the clinic, waiting around, or even at home if I’m trying to just sit and relax. It’s not relaxing to me. The longer I have to be still, the more worked up I get, and then I start thinking, “I need to stand up, I need to walk, I need to do something.” If I can’t, then yeah, I get worried and tense, like I’m gonna crawl out of my own skin.\n\nSo it’s not always worry like I’m scared of something bad happening. It’s more like my body gets so restless that my mind starts getting anxious with it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind that you can't stop thinking about?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 36 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure… I mean, I don’t think I have thoughts that repeat like that, not in the way you’re asking maybe.

Like, I can get something on my mind and it’ll bother me for a bit, or I’ll keep thinking about what I need to do, or something somebody said, but I don’t know if I’d call it thoughts I can’t stop. It’s more like my mind jumps around a lot, if that makes sense. But I’m not sitting there with one same thought going over and over and over where I feel like I have to do something about it.

Unless you mean like worrying? Because I do think about stuff, but I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed anything like a thought getting stuck in my head the same way my body gets stuck needing to move. It’s more physical for me, from what I can tell.

I’ll tell myself, “Just sit there, just be still,” but then my legs want to move or I feel like I need to stand up, walk, go outside, anything. I’m not working right now, but it still affects me bad with people. And I want to help, I really do, but I guess I’ve messed it up enough times that now folks don’t really trust me with it.\n\nIt’s like they’ll say, “No, we got somebody else,” or they won’t ask me anymore, and I can feel it. Like… it ain’t just “I’m a little uncomfortable.” It feels like something is crawling under my skin, or like there’s a motor in me that won’t shut off. If I’m sitting at church, or waiting here at the clinic, or even just trying to sit at home, I start feeling trapped in my own body.\n\nI can try to tell myself, “Stay still, don’t make a big deal out of it,” but the longer I have to sit there, the worse it gets. My legs start wanting to move, I start looking for a reason to stand up, or I’ll feel like I need to go outside or mess with something just to take the pressure off. It builds up until it’s all I can think about.\n\nSo yeah, it bothers me bad. It makes me feel tense and frustrated, like I’m fighting myself just to do something simple like sit still.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not like some big ritual or anything, but I’m always doing little things over and over without even meaning to. Like even when I’m trying to sit there and be respectful — like in church, or sitting across from somebody talking — my leg will be bouncing the whole time. And I mean the whole time, until somebody looks at it or I notice the chair shaking.\n\nOr my fingers will be tapping on the arm of the chair, or on my knee, or I’ll be picking at something, twisting something in my hands, messing with a cap or a piece of paper or whatever I can get hold of. I’ll just notice I’m bouncing my leg or rubbing my hands, and if I try to stop, then I get all tense and uncomfortable.\n\nSo no, I don’t think it’s about preventing something. It’s more just… I can’t seem to keep still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried when you're unable to move around or be still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get anxious, but it’s tied to that stuck feeling, you know? Like if I’m not able to move around or get up, it starts feeling like I’m trapped. That’s really why I came in, because it feels like I got a motor running inside me all day, and it don’t shut off just because I’m supposed to be sitting still.\n\nLike at church, if everybody’s sitting quiet and I know I’m supposed to stay put, I start feeling all tight inside. Same thing here at the clinic, waiting around, or even at home if I’m trying to just sit and relax. It’s not relaxing to me. The longer I have to be still, the more worked up I get, and then I start thinking, “I need to stand up, I need to walk, I need to do something.” If I can’t, then yeah, I get worried and tense, like I’m gonna crawl out of my own skin.\n\nSo it’s not always worry like I’m scared of something bad happening. It’s more like my body gets so restless that my mind starts getting anxious with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I mean, I don’t think I have thoughts that repeat like that, not in the way you’re asking maybe.\n\nLike, I can get something on my mind and it’ll bother me for a bit, or I’ll keep thinking about what I need to do, or something somebody said, but I don’t know if I’d call it thoughts I can’t stop. It’s more like my mind jumps around a lot, if that makes sense. But I’m not sitting there with one same thought going over and over and over where I feel like I have to do something about it.\n\nUnless you mean like worrying? Because I do think about stuff, but I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed anything like a thought getting stuck in my head the same way my body gets stuck needing to move. It’s more physical for me, from what I can tell.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you have trouble sitting still or feel excessively restless when you were a kid?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 36 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Back when I was a boy in school, teachers were always moving my seat because I could not stay put, talked out of turn, and got in trouble for jumping up before class was done.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… definitely. That was kind of me all through school, from what I remember. I was always getting in trouble for not staying in my seat. Teachers would move me around the classroom, like closer to their desk or away from certain kids, because I’d be turning around, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, messing with stuff, getting up before I was supposed to.

I remember I’d just pop up like class was over, even when it wasn’t. Like I’d decide I needed to sharpen a pencil, or go throw something away, or just stand up, and the teacher would be like, “Sit back down, we’re not done.” It wasn’t like I was trying to be bad all the time. I just couldn’t seem to keep myself planted there.

And talking out of turn was a big one too. I’d blurt things out, answer before I was called on, talk to the kid next to me, and then I’d get my seat moved again. That happened a lot. So yeah, even as a boy, sitting still was a real problem for me.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I just can’t seem to be still. I’m not working right now, but it still affects me bad with people. Like… it ain’t just “I’m a little uncomfortable.” It feels like something is crawling under my skin, or like there’s a motor in me that won’t shut off. If I’m sitting at church, or waiting here at the clinic, or even just trying to sit at home, I start feeling trapped in my own body.\n\nI can try to tell myself, “Stay still, don’t make a big deal out of it,” but the longer I have to sit there, the worse it gets. It’s more like my mind jumps around a lot, if that makes sense. But I’m not sitting there with one same thought going over and over and over where I feel like I have to do something about it.\n\nUnless you mean like worrying? Because I do think about stuff, but I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed anything like a thought getting stuck in my head the same way my body gets stuck needing to move. It’s more physical for me, from what I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or feel excessively restless when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That was kind of me all through school, from what I remember. I was always getting in trouble for not staying in my seat. Teachers would move me around the classroom, like closer to their desk or away from certain kids, because I’d be turning around, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, messing with stuff, getting up before I was supposed to.\n\nI remember I’d just pop up like class was over, even when it wasn’t. Like I’d decide I needed to sharpen a pencil, or go throw something away, or just stand up, and the teacher would be like, “Sit back down, we’re not done.” It wasn’t like I was trying to be bad all the time. I just couldn’t seem to keep myself planted there.\n\nAnd talking out of turn was a big one too. I’d blurt things out, answer before I was called on, talk to the kid next to me, and then I’d get my seat moved again. That happened a lot. So yeah, even as a boy, sitting still was a real problem for me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you have trouble sitting still or feel restless before you were 7 years old?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so. I mean, I was real young when that started. I can’t give you an exact age like, “Oh, I was six,” but back when I was a boy in school, even early on, teachers were always moving my seat because I couldn’t stay put.

I’d be talking when I wasn’t supposed to, turning around, messing around, and then I’d jump up before class was done like I had somewhere to go. The teacher would be like, “Sit down, class isn’t over,” and I’d get in trouble for it. That happened enough that it wasn’t just one teacher having a problem with me. It was kind of a regular thing. So yeah, before I was very old, sitting still was already hard for me.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I just can’t seem to be still. It’s like I got a motor running inside me all day, from when I’m up until I’m trying to sit down somewhere. If I’m at church, or sitting here at the clinic waiting, or even just at home, I start feeling trapped in my own body, like I gotta get up, move around, mess with something, do something.\n\nIt’s not just being bored, you know? It feels uncomfortable, like pressure building up if I have to stay sitting. I’ll tell myself, “Just sit there, just be still,” but then my legs want to move or I feel like I need to stand up, walk, go outside, anything. It’s been wearing me out, and I figured I better come talk to somebody because it’s all day, every day kind of feeling.\n\nDoctor: Is this restlessness and need to move affecting your work or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it is. I’m not working right now, but it still affects me bad with people. Like at church, and at the pantry, and with my own family, people used to ask me to help with rides, or getting somebody to an appointment, or picking up supplies, stuff like that. And I want to help, I really do, but I guess I’ve messed it up enough times that now folks don’t really trust me with it.\n\nIt’s like they’ll say, “No, we got somebody else,” or they won’t ask me anymore, and I can feel it. My family too — they’ll kind of go around me when something important needs doing. That hurts, because I don’t mean to let anybody down. I say yes because I want to be useful, but then somehow it turns into me being the one who didn’t come through, and everybody’s frustrated.\n\nSo yeah, it’s affecting relationships. I feel like I keep disappointing people even when my heart is in the right place.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to move around or be still?\n\nPatient: It’s a lot of distress, honestly. Like… it ain’t just “I’m a little uncomfortable.” It feels like something is crawling under my skin, or like there’s a motor in me that won’t shut off. If I’m sitting at church, or waiting here at the clinic, or even just trying to sit at home, I start feeling trapped in my own body.\n\nI can try to tell myself, “Stay still, don’t make a big deal out of it,” but the longer I have to sit there, the worse it gets. My legs start wanting to move, I start looking for a reason to stand up, or I’ll feel like I need to go outside or mess with something just to take the pressure off. It builds up until it’s all I can think about.\n\nSo yeah, it bothers me bad. It makes me feel tense and frustrated, like I’m fighting myself just to do something simple like sit still.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not like some big ritual or anything, but I’m always doing little things over and over without even meaning to. Like even when I’m trying to sit there and be respectful — like in church, or sitting across from somebody talking — my leg will be bouncing the whole time. And I mean the whole time, until somebody looks at it or I notice the chair shaking.\n\nOr my fingers will be tapping on the arm of the chair, or on my knee, or I’ll be picking at something, twisting something in my hands, messing with a cap or a piece of paper or whatever I can get hold of. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until someone says, “Can you stop that?” And then I’ll stop for a minute, but it’s like my body finds another way to move. If the leg stops, then the fingers start. If the fingers stop, I’m twisting my hands or rubbing them together.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I really am trying not to bother nobody. I’m not doing it to be rude. It’s just like I can’t keep my body quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive movements because you're trying to make something right or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not like that. It’s not like I’m thinking, “If I don’t tap my fingers, something bad’s gonna happen,” or I’m trying to fix something in my head.\n\nIt’s more like… my body just does it. Like pressure building up, and the movement lets a little of it out. I’m not trying to make anything right, really. I’m not counting or doing it a certain number of times or anything like that. It’s more just… I can’t seem to keep still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried when you're unable to move around or be still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get anxious, but it’s tied to that stuck feeling, you know? Like if I’m not able to move around or get up, it starts feeling like I’m trapped. That’s really why I came in, because it feels like I got a motor running inside me all day, and it don’t shut off just because I’m supposed to be sitting still.\n\nLike at church, if everybody’s sitting quiet and I know I’m supposed to stay put, I start feeling all tight inside. Same thing here at the clinic, waiting around, or even at home if I’m trying to just sit and relax. It’s not relaxing to me. The longer I have to be still, the more worked up I get, and then I start thinking, “I need to stand up, I need to walk, I need to do something.” If I can’t, then yeah, I get worried and tense, like I’m gonna crawl out of my own skin.\n\nSo it’s not always worry like I’m scared of something bad happening. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, paying attention was hard too, I think. Not always in the sense that I didn’t care, because I’d try to listen at first. Like if somebody was telling me something I needed to know, I’d be looking at them and hearing the first part, but then my mind would just kind of slip off somewhere else.

Even now I can remember that kind of thing with grown-ups talking through plans — like ride times, or what we were supposed to get from the pantry, or church stuff, where we were supposed to be and when. I’d catch the beginning, and then by the time they got to the part I really needed, I’d realize I missed pieces of it. Then I’d be standing there like, “Wait, what time did you say?” or “What was I supposed to bring?” And people would get frustrated because they had just told me.

So yeah, following instructions could be hard if there was too much said at once. I wasn’t trying to ignore people. It was more like I’d start out listening good, and then my head would wander off before I knew it, and I’d lose the important part.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I just can’t seem to be still. It’s like I got a motor running inside me all day, from when I’m up until I’m trying to sit down somewhere. If I’m at church, or sitting here at the clinic waiting, or even just at home, I start feeling trapped in my own body, like I gotta get up, move around, mess with something, do something.\n\nIt’s not just being bored, you know? It feels uncomfortable, like pressure building up if I have to stay sitting. I’ll tell myself, “Just sit there, just be still,” but then my legs want to move or I feel like I need to stand up, walk, go outside, anything. It’s been wearing me out, and I figured I better come talk to somebody because it’s all day, every day kind of feeling.\n\nDoctor: Is this restlessness and need to move affecting your work or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it is. I’m not working right now, but it still affects me bad with people. Like at church, and at the pantry, and with my own family, people used to ask me to help with rides, or getting somebody to an appointment, or picking up supplies, stuff like that. And I want to help, I really do, but I guess I’ve messed it up enough times that now folks don’t really trust me with it.\n\nIt’s like they’ll say, “No, we got somebody else,” or they won’t ask me anymore, and I can feel it. My family too — they’ll kind of go around me when something important needs doing. That hurts, because I don’t mean to let anybody down. I say yes because I want to be useful, but then somehow it turns into me being the one who didn’t come through, and everybody’s frustrated.\n\nSo yeah, it’s affecting relationships. I feel like I keep disappointing people even when my heart is in the right place.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to move around or be still?\n\nPatient: It’s a lot of distress, honestly. Like… it ain’t just “I’m a little uncomfortable.” It feels like something is crawling under my skin, or like there’s a motor in me that won’t shut off. If I’m sitting at church, or waiting here at the clinic, or even just trying to sit at home, I start feeling trapped in my own body.\n\nI can try to tell myself, “Stay still, don’t make a big deal out of it,” but the longer I have to sit there, the worse it gets. My legs start wanting to move, I start looking for a reason to stand up, or I’ll feel like I need to go outside or mess with something just to take the pressure off. It builds up until it’s all I can think about.\n\nSo yeah, it bothers me bad. It makes me feel tense and frustrated, like I’m fighting myself just to do something simple like sit still.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not like some big ritual or anything, but I’m always doing little things over and over without even meaning to. Like even when I’m trying to sit there and be respectful — like in church, or sitting across from somebody talking — my leg will be bouncing the whole time. And I mean the whole time, until somebody looks at it or I notice the chair shaking.\n\nOr my fingers will be tapping on the arm of the chair, or on my knee, or I’ll be picking at something, twisting something in my hands, messing with a cap or a piece of paper or whatever I can get hold of. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until someone says, “Can you stop that?” And then I’ll stop for a minute, but it’s like my body finds another way to move. If the leg stops, then the fingers start. If the fingers stop, I’m twisting my hands or rubbing them together.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I really am trying not to bother nobody. I’m not doing it to be rude. It’s just like I can’t keep my body quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive movements because you're trying to make something right or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not like that. It’s not like I’m thinking, “If I don’t tap my fingers, something bad’s gonna happen,” or I’m trying to fix something in my head.\n\nIt’s more like… my body just does it. Like pressure building up, and the movement lets a little of it out. I’m not trying to make anything right, really. I’m not counting or doing it a certain number of times or anything like that. I’ll just notice I’m bouncing my leg or rubbing my hands, and if I try to stop, then I get all tense and uncomfortable.\n\nSo no, I don’t think it’s about preventing something. It’s more just… I can’t seem to keep still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried when you're unable to move around or be still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get anxious, but it’s tied to that stuck feeling, you know? Like if I’m not able to move around or get up, it starts feeling like I’m trapped. That’s really why I came in, because it feels like I got a motor running inside me all day, and it don’t shut off just because I’m supposed to be sitting still.\n\nLike at church, if everybody’s sitting quiet and I know I’m supposed to stay put, I start feeling all tight inside. Same thing here at the clinic, waiting around, or even at home if I’m trying to just sit and relax. It’s not relaxing to me. The longer I have to be still, the more worked up I get, and then I start thinking, “I need to stand up, I need to walk, I need to do something.” If I can’t, then yeah, I get worried and tense, like I’m gonna crawl out of my own skin.\n\nSo it’s not always worry like I’m scared of something bad happening. It’s more like my body gets so restless that my mind starts getting anxious with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I mean, I don’t think I have thoughts that repeat like that, not in the way you’re asking maybe.\n\nLike, I can get something on my mind and it’ll bother me for a bit, or I’ll keep thinking about what I need to do, or something somebody said, but I don’t know if I’d call it thoughts I can’t stop. It’s more like my mind jumps around a lot, if that makes sense. But I’m not sitting there with one same thought going over and over and over where I feel like I have to do something about it.\n\nUnless you mean like worrying? Because I do think about stuff, but I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed anything like a thought getting stuck in my head the same way my body gets stuck needing to move. It’s more physical for me, from what I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or feel excessively restless when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That was kind of me all through school, from what I remember. I was always getting in trouble for not staying in my seat. Like I’d decide I needed to sharpen a pencil, or go throw something away, or just stand up, and the teacher would be like, “Sit back down, we’re not done.” It wasn’t like I was trying to be bad all the time. I just couldn’t seem to keep myself planted there.\n\nAnd talking out of turn was a big one too. I’d blurt things out, answer before I was called on, talk to the kid next to me, and then I’d get my seat moved again. That happened a lot. So yeah, even as a boy, sitting still was a real problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or feel restless before you were 7 years old?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I was real young when that started. I can’t give you an exact age like, “Oh, I was six,” but back when I was a boy in school, even early on, teachers were always moving my seat because I couldn’t stay put.\n\nI’d be talking when I wasn’t supposed to, turning around, messing around, and then I’d jump up before class was done like I had somewhere to go. The teacher would be like, “Sit down, class isn’t over,” and I’d get in trouble for it. That happened enough that it wasn’t just one teacher having a problem with me. It was kind of a regular thing. So yeah, before I was very old, sitting still was already hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any other symptoms or difficulties as a child, such as problems with paying attention or following instructions?\n\nPatient: Yeah, paying attention was hard too, I think. Not always in the sense that I didn’t care, because I’d try to listen at first. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until someone says, “Can you stop that?” And then I’ll stop for a minute, but it’s like my body finds another way to move. If the leg stops, then the fingers start. If the fingers stop, I’m twisting my hands or rubbing them together.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I really am trying not to bother nobody. I’m not doing it to be rude. It’s just like I can’t keep my body quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive movements because you're trying to make something right or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not like that. It’s not like I’m thinking, “If I don’t tap my fingers, something bad’s gonna happen,” or I’m trying to fix something in my head.\n\nIt’s more like… my body just does it. Like pressure building up, and the movement lets a little of it out. I’m not trying to make anything right, really. I’m not counting or doing it a certain number of times or anything like that. I’ll just notice I’m bouncing my leg or rubbing my hands, and if I try to stop, then I get all tense and uncomfortable.\n\nSo no, I don’t think it’s about preventing something. It’s more just… I can’t seem to keep still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried when you're unable to move around or be still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get anxious, but it’s tied to that stuck feeling, you know? Like if I’m not able to move around or get up, it starts feeling like I’m trapped. That’s really why I came in, because it feels like I got a motor running inside me all day, and it don’t shut off just because I’m supposed to be sitting still.\n\nLike at church, if everybody’s sitting quiet and I know I’m supposed to stay put, I start feeling all tight inside. Same thing here at the clinic, waiting around, or even at home if I’m trying to just sit and relax. It’s not relaxing to me. The longer I have to be still, the more worked up I get, and then I start thinking, “I need to stand up, I need to walk, I need to do something.” If I can’t, then yeah, I get worried and tense, like I’m gonna crawl out of my own skin.\n\nSo it’s not always worry like I’m scared of something bad happening. It’s more like my body gets so restless that my mind starts getting anxious with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I mean, I don’t think I have thoughts that repeat like that, not in the way you’re asking maybe.\n\nLike, I can get something on my mind and it’ll bother me for a bit, or I’ll keep thinking about what I need to do, or something somebody said, but I don’t know if I’d call it thoughts I can’t stop. It’s more like my mind jumps around a lot, if that makes sense. But I’m not sitting there with one same thought going over and over and over where I feel like I have to do something about it.\n\nUnless you mean like worrying? Because I do think about stuff, but I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed anything like a thought getting stuck in my head the same way my body gets stuck needing to move. It’s more physical for me, from what I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or feel excessively restless when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That was kind of me all through school, from what I remember. I was always getting in trouble for not staying in my seat. Teachers would move me around the classroom, like closer to their desk or away from certain kids, because I’d be turning around, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, messing with stuff, getting up before I was supposed to.\n\nI remember I’d just pop up like class was over, even when it wasn’t. Like I’d decide I needed to sharpen a pencil, or go throw something away, or just stand up, and the teacher would be like, “Sit back down, we’re not done.” It wasn’t like I was trying to be bad all the time. I just couldn’t seem to keep myself planted there.\n\nAnd talking out of turn was a big one too. I’d blurt things out, answer before I was called on, talk to the kid next to me, and then I’d get my seat moved again. That happened a lot. So yeah, even as a boy, sitting still was a real problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or feel restless before you were 7 years old?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I was real young when that started. I can’t give you an exact age like, “Oh, I was six,” but back when I was a boy in school, even early on, teachers were always moving my seat because I couldn’t stay put.\n\nI’d be talking when I wasn’t supposed to, turning around, messing around, and then I’d jump up before class was done like I had somewhere to go. The teacher would be like, “Sit down, class isn’t over,” and I’d get in trouble for it. That happened enough that it wasn’t just one teacher having a problem with me. It was kind of a regular thing. So yeah, before I was very old, sitting still was already hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any other symptoms or difficulties as a child, such as problems with paying attention or following instructions?\n\nPatient: Yeah, paying attention was hard too, I think. Not always in the sense that I didn’t care, because I’d try to listen at first. Like if somebody was telling me something I needed to know, I’d be looking at them and hearing the first part, but then my mind would just kind of slip off somewhere else.\n\nEven now I can remember that kind of thing with grown-ups talking through plans — like ride times, or what we were supposed to get from the pantry, or church stuff, where we were supposed to be and when. I’d catch the beginning, and then by the time they got to the part I really needed, I’d realize I missed pieces of it. Then I’d be standing there like, “Wait, what time did you say?” or “What was I supposed to bring?” And people would get frustrated because they had just told me.\n\nSo yeah, following instructions could be hard if there was too much said at once. I wasn’t trying to ignore people. It was more like I’d start out listening good, and then my head would wander off before I knew it, and I’d lose the important part.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yeah, I do. A lot of times I’ll answer before somebody is even done saying what they’re saying. Like with the pastor, or my cousin, or the lady at the pantry, they’ll be explaining something, and I’ll jump in because I think I already know what they’re about to ask or what they mean.

But then right after I say it, I realize I answered the wrong thing, or I cut them off, or I made them irritated. Like they’ll look at me like, “Let me finish,” and then I feel stupid because if I had just waited two more seconds, I would’ve understood it better. It’s like my mouth gets there before my brain catches up.

I don’t always mean to be rude. It just comes out fast. And then I’m stuck trying to fix it, like, “Oh, sorry, I thought you meant…” but by then they’re already annoyed. So yeah, I’d say I act before thinking, especially with talking and answering too quick.

If I’m sitting at church, or waiting here at the clinic, or even just trying to sit at home, I start feeling trapped in my own body.\n\nI can try to tell myself, “Stay still, don’t make a big deal out of it,” but the longer I have to sit there, the worse it gets. My legs start wanting to move, I start looking for a reason to stand up, or I’ll feel like I need to go outside or mess with something just to take the pressure off. It builds up until it’s all I can think about.\n\nSo yeah, it bothers me bad. It makes me feel tense and frustrated, like I’m fighting myself just to do something simple like sit still.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not like some big ritual or anything, but I’m always doing little things over and over without even meaning to. Like even when I’m trying to sit there and be respectful — like in church, or sitting across from somebody talking — my leg will be bouncing the whole time. And I mean the whole time, until somebody looks at it or I notice the chair shaking.\n\nOr my fingers will be tapping on the arm of the chair, or on my knee, or I’ll be picking at something, twisting something in my hands, messing with a cap or a piece of paper or whatever I can get hold of. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until someone says, “Can you stop that?” And then I’ll stop for a minute, but it’s like my body finds another way to move. If the leg stops, then the fingers start. If the fingers stop, I’m twisting my hands or rubbing them together.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I really am trying not to bother nobody. I’m not doing it to be rude. It’s just like I can’t keep my body quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive movements because you're trying to make something right or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not like that. It’s not like I’m thinking, “If I don’t tap my fingers, something bad’s gonna happen,” or I’m trying to fix something in my head.\n\nIt’s more like… my body just does it. Like pressure building up, and the movement lets a little of it out. I’m not trying to make anything right, really. I’m not counting or doing it a certain number of times or anything like that. I’ll just notice I’m bouncing my leg or rubbing my hands, and if I try to stop, then I get all tense and uncomfortable.\n\nSo no, I don’t think it’s about preventing something. It’s more just… I can’t seem to keep still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried when you're unable to move around or be still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get anxious, but it’s tied to that stuck feeling, you know? Like if I’m not able to move around or get up, it starts feeling like I’m trapped. That’s really why I came in, because it feels like I got a motor running inside me all day, and it don’t shut off just because I’m supposed to be sitting still.\n\nLike at church, if everybody’s sitting quiet and I know I’m supposed to stay put, I start feeling all tight inside. Same thing here at the clinic, waiting around, or even at home if I’m trying to just sit and relax. It’s not relaxing to me. The longer I have to be still, the more worked up I get, and then I start thinking, “I need to stand up, I need to walk, I need to do something.” If I can’t, then yeah, I get worried and tense, like I’m gonna crawl out of my own skin.\n\nSo it’s not always worry like I’m scared of something bad happening. It’s more like my body gets so restless that my mind starts getting anxious with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I mean, I don’t think I have thoughts that repeat like that, not in the way you’re asking maybe.\n\nLike, I can get something on my mind and it’ll bother me for a bit, or I’ll keep thinking about what I need to do, or something somebody said, but I don’t know if I’d call it thoughts I can’t stop. It’s more like my mind jumps around a lot, if that makes sense. But I’m not sitting there with one same thought going over and over and over where I feel like I have to do something about it.\n\nUnless you mean like worrying? Because I do think about stuff, but I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed anything like a thought getting stuck in my head the same way my body gets stuck needing to move. It’s more physical for me, from what I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or feel excessively restless when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That was kind of me all through school, from what I remember. I was always getting in trouble for not staying in my seat. Teachers would move me around the classroom, like closer to their desk or away from certain kids, because I’d be turning around, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, messing with stuff, getting up before I was supposed to.\n\nI remember I’d just pop up like class was over, even when it wasn’t. Like I’d decide I needed to sharpen a pencil, or go throw something away, or just stand up, and the teacher would be like, “Sit back down, we’re not done.” It wasn’t like I was trying to be bad all the time. I just couldn’t seem to keep myself planted there.\n\nAnd talking out of turn was a big one too. I’d blurt things out, answer before I was called on, talk to the kid next to me, and then I’d get my seat moved again. That happened a lot. So yeah, even as a boy, sitting still was a real problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or feel restless before you were 7 years old?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I was real young when that started. I can’t give you an exact age like, “Oh, I was six,” but back when I was a boy in school, even early on, teachers were always moving my seat because I couldn’t stay put.\n\nI’d be talking when I wasn’t supposed to, turning around, messing around, and then I’d jump up before class was done like I had somewhere to go. The teacher would be like, “Sit down, class isn’t over,” and I’d get in trouble for it. That happened enough that it wasn’t just one teacher having a problem with me. It was kind of a regular thing. So yeah, before I was very old, sitting still was already hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any other symptoms or difficulties as a child, such as problems with paying attention or following instructions?\n\nPatient: Yeah, paying attention was hard too, I think. Not always in the sense that I didn’t care, because I’d try to listen at first. Like if somebody was telling me something I needed to know, I’d be looking at them and hearing the first part, but then my mind would just kind of slip off somewhere else.\n\nEven now I can remember that kind of thing with grown-ups talking through plans — like ride times, or what we were supposed to get from the pantry, or church stuff, where we were supposed to be and when. I’d catch the beginning, and then by the time they got to the part I really needed, I’d realize I missed pieces of it. Then I’d be standing there like, “Wait, what time did you say?” or “What was I supposed to bring?” And people would get frustrated because they had just told me.\n\nSo yeah, following instructions could be hard if there was too much said at once. I wasn’t trying to ignore people. It was more like I’d start out listening good, and then my head would wander off before I knew it, and I’d lose the important part.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. A lot of times I’ll answer before somebody is even done saying what they’re saying. Like with the pastor, or my cousin, or the lady at the pantry, they’ll be explaining something, and I’ll jump in because I think I already know what they’re about to ask or what they mean.\n\nBut then right after I say it, I realize I answered the wrong thing, or I cut them off, or I made them irritated. Like they’ll look at me like, “Let me finish,” and then I feel stupid because if I had just waited two more seconds, I would’ve understood it better. It’s like my mouth gets there before my brain catches up.\n\nI don’t always mean to be rude. It just comes out fast. And then I’m stuck trying to fix it, like, “Oh, sorry, I thought you meant…” but by then they’re already annoyed. So yeah, I’d say I act before thinking, especially with talking and answering too quick.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more like too much energy inside me, like I got a motor running in me all day. That’s the best way I can say it. It’s not always that I don’t know what’s going on around me — sometimes I know exactly where I am and what I’m supposed to be doing — but my body just don’t want to stay there.

Like at church, if I’m sitting in the pew too long, I start feeling trapped, like I gotta move my legs or shift around or stand up for some reason. Same thing here at the clinic, even waiting, I can feel myself getting worked up just sitting there. At home too, if I’m supposed to just sit quiet, it feels almost uncomfortable, like something in me is pushing me to get up and do something, anything.

So yeah, I’d say the staying still part feels more like that restless energy, like I can’t turn it off. The focus part is there sometimes too, but when you ask about sitting still and acting quick, it really feels like my body is already moving before I’ve had a chance to think it through.

---------- turn 12 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.0, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I just can’t seem to be still. It’s like I got a motor running inside me all day, from when I’m up until I’m trying to sit down somewhere. If I’m at church, or sitting here at the clinic waiting, or even just at home, I start feeling trapped in my own body, like I gotta get up, move around, mess with something, do something.\n\nIt’s not just being bored, you know? It feels uncomfortable, like pressure building up if I have to stay sitting. I’ll tell myself, “Just sit there, just be still,” but then my legs want to move or I feel like I need to stand up, walk, go outside, anything. It’s been wearing me out, and I figured I better come talk to somebody because it’s all day, every day kind of feeling.\n\nDoctor: Is this restlessness and need to move affecting your work or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it is. I’m not working right now, but it still affects me bad with people. Like at church, and at the pantry, and with my own family, people used to ask me to help with rides, or getting somebody to an appointment, or picking up supplies, stuff like that. And I want to help, I really do, but I guess I’ve messed it up enough times that now folks don’t really trust me with it.\n\nIt’s like they’ll say, “No, we got somebody else,” or they won’t ask me anymore, and I can feel it. My family too — they’ll kind of go around me when something important needs doing. That hurts, because I don’t mean to let anybody down. I say yes because I want to be useful, but then somehow it turns into me being the one who didn’t come through, and everybody’s frustrated.\n\nSo yeah, it’s affecting relationships. I feel like I keep disappointing people even when my heart is in the right place.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to move around or be still?\n\nPatient: It’s a lot of distress, honestly. Like… it ain’t just “I’m a little uncomfortable.” It feels like something is crawling under my skin, or like there’s a motor in me that won’t shut off. If I’m sitting at church, or waiting here at the clinic, or even just trying to sit at home, I start feeling trapped in my own body.\n\nI can try to tell myself, “Stay still, don’t make a big deal out of it,” but the longer I have to sit there, the worse it gets. My legs start wanting to move, I start looking for a reason to stand up, or I’ll feel like I need to go outside or mess with something just to take the pressure off. It builds up until it’s all I can think about.\n\nSo yeah, it bothers me bad. It makes me feel tense and frustrated, like I’m fighting myself just to do something simple like sit still.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not like some big ritual or anything, but I’m always doing little things over and over without even meaning to. Like even when I’m trying to sit there and be respectful — like in church, or sitting across from somebody talking — my leg will be bouncing the whole time. And I mean the whole time, until somebody looks at it or I notice the chair shaking.\n\nOr my fingers will be tapping on the arm of the chair, or on my knee, or I’ll be picking at something, twisting something in my hands, messing with a cap or a piece of paper or whatever I can get hold of. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until someone says, “Can you stop that?” And then I’ll stop for a minute, but it’s like my body finds another way to move. If the leg stops, then the fingers start. If the fingers stop, I’m twisting my hands or rubbing them together.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I really am trying not to bother nobody. I’m not doing it to be rude. It’s just like I can’t keep my body quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive movements because you're trying to make something right or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not like that. It’s not like I’m thinking, “If I don’t tap my fingers, something bad’s gonna happen,” or I’m trying to fix something in my head.\n\nIt’s more like… my body just does it. Like pressure building up, and the movement lets a little of it out. I’m not trying to make anything right, really. I’m not counting or doing it a certain number of times or anything like that. I’ll just notice I’m bouncing my leg or rubbing my hands, and if I try to stop, then I get all tense and uncomfortable.\n\nSo no, I don’t think it’s about preventing something. It’s more just… I can’t seem to keep still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried when you're unable to move around or be still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get anxious, but it’s tied to that stuck feeling, you know? Like if I’m not able to move around or get up, it starts feeling like I’m trapped. That’s really why I came in, because it feels like I got a motor running inside me all day, and it don’t shut off just because I’m supposed to be sitting still.\n\nLike at church, if everybody’s sitting quiet and I know I’m supposed to stay put, I start feeling all tight inside. Same thing here at the clinic, waiting around, or even at home if I’m trying to just sit and relax. It’s not relaxing to me. The longer I have to be still, the more worked up I get, and then I start thinking, “I need to stand up, I need to walk, I need to do something.” If I can’t, then yeah, I get worried and tense, like I’m gonna crawl out of my own skin.\n\nSo it’s not always worry like I’m scared of something bad happening. It’s more like my body gets so restless that my mind starts getting anxious with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I mean, I don’t think I have thoughts that repeat like that, not in the way you’re asking maybe.\n\nLike, I can get something on my mind and it’ll bother me for a bit, or I’ll keep thinking about what I need to do, or something somebody said, but I don’t know if I’d call it thoughts I can’t stop. It’s more like my mind jumps around a lot, if that makes sense. But I’m not sitting there with one same thought going over and over and over where I feel like I have to do something about it.\n\nUnless you mean like worrying? Because I do think about stuff, but I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed anything like a thought getting stuck in my head the same way my body gets stuck needing to move. It’s more physical for me, from what I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or feel excessively restless when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That was kind of me all through school, from what I remember. I was always getting in trouble for not staying in my seat. Teachers would move me around the classroom, like closer to their desk or away from certain kids, because I’d be turning around, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, messing with stuff, getting up before I was supposed to.\n\nI remember I’d just pop up like class was over, even when it wasn’t. Like I’d decide I needed to sharpen a pencil, or go throw something away, or just stand up, and the teacher would be like, “Sit back down, we’re not done.” It wasn’t like I was trying to be bad all the time. I just couldn’t seem to keep myself planted there.\n\nAnd talking out of turn was a big one too. I’d blurt things out, answer before I was called on, talk to the kid next to me, and then I’d get my seat moved again. That happened a lot. So yeah, even as a boy, sitting still was a real problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or feel restless before you were 7 years old?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I was real young when that started. I can’t give you an exact age like, “Oh, I was six,” but back when I was a boy in school, even early on, teachers were always moving my seat because I couldn’t stay put.\n\nI’d be talking when I wasn’t supposed to, turning around, messing around, and then I’d jump up before class was done like I had somewhere to go. The teacher would be like, “Sit down, class isn’t over,” and I’d get in trouble for it. That happened enough that it wasn’t just one teacher having a problem with me. It was kind of a regular thing. So yeah, before I was very old, sitting still was already hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any other symptoms or difficulties as a child, such as problems with paying attention or following instructions?\n\nPatient: Yeah, paying attention was hard too, I think. Not always in the sense that I didn’t care, because I’d try to listen at first. Like if somebody was telling me something I needed to know, I’d be looking at them and hearing the first part, but then my mind would just kind of slip off somewhere else.\n\nEven now I can remember that kind of thing with grown-ups talking through plans — like ride times, or what we were supposed to get from the pantry, or church stuff, where we were supposed to be and when. I’d catch the beginning, and then by the time they got to the part I really needed, I’d realize I missed pieces of it. Then I’d be standing there like, “Wait, what time did you say?” or “What was I supposed to bring?” And people would get frustrated because they had just told me.\n\nSo yeah, following instructions could be hard if there was too much said at once. I wasn’t trying to ignore people. It was more like I’d start out listening good, and then my head would wander off before I knew it, and I’d lose the important part.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. A lot of times I’ll answer before somebody is even done saying what they’re saying. Like with the pastor, or my cousin, or the lady at the pantry, they’ll be explaining something, and I’ll jump in because I think I already know what they’re about to ask or what they mean.\n\nBut then right after I say it, I realize I answered the wrong thing, or I cut them off, or I made them irritated. Like they’ll look at me like, “Let me finish,” and then I feel stupid because if I had just waited two more seconds, I would’ve understood it better. It’s like my mouth gets there before my brain catches up.\n\nI don’t always mean to be rude. It just comes out fast. And then I’m stuck trying to fix it, like, “Oh, sorry, I thought you meant…” but by then they’re already annoyed. So yeah, I’d say I act before thinking, especially with talking and answering too quick.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying still and acting on impulse are more related to having too much energy or having trouble focusing on what's going on around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like too much energy inside me, like I got a motor running in me all day. That’s the best way I can say it. It’s not always that I don’t know what’s going on around me — sometimes I know exactly where I am and what I’m supposed to be doing — but my body just don’t want to stay there.\n\nLike at church, if I’m sitting in the pew too long, I start feeling trapped, like I gotta move my legs or shift around or stand up for some reason. Same thing here at the clinic, even waiting, I can feel myself getting worked up just sitting there. At home too, if I’m supposed to just sit quiet, it feels almost uncomfortable, like something in me is pushing me to get up and do something, anything.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say the staying still part feels more like that restless energy, like I can’t turn it off. The focus part is there sometimes too, but when you ask about sitting still and acting quick, it really feels like my body is already moving before I’ve had a chance to think it through.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… maybe a little, if it really grabs me, but not like it goes away. That’s kind of why I came in, because it feels like I got a motor running inside me all day. Even if I’m trying to pay attention, my body still feels like it wants to move.

Like at church, I can be trying to listen, and I know I’m supposed to sit there, but after a while I start feeling trapped in the pew. I’ll shift my legs, lean forward, sit back, look around, anything just to not feel stuck. Same thing at the clinic when I’m waiting — I know I’m there for a reason, I know I need to sit, but it’s like something inside me keeps pushing me to get up.

And at home too, if I’m supposed to just sit still, it feels uncomfortable. I’ll start thinking, “I need to do something,” even if there’s nothing I really need to do. So if something takes my full attention, maybe I can hold still a little better for a short time, but that restless feeling is still there underneath. It’s not just boredom to me. It’s more like my body won’t shut off.
